‘The Rings of Power’ Season 3 Review: Amazon’s ‘Lord of the Rings’ Series Is Just Going Through the Motions

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The charges against Amazon’s epic (and epically priced) “Lord of the Rings” series, “The Rings of Power,” stacked up quickly. The first two seasons were pretty good by blockbuster standards — and I wasn’t alone in saying so — but the record-breaking costs, as well as the franchise’s lofty pedigree, set expectations higher than Great Eagles can soar. When the most expensive TV series ever made struggled to yield the requisite viewership spikes and comparable cultural impact, the claws came out.

Showrunners J.D. Payne and Patrick McKay’s series was, among other prominent complaints, a lackluster imitation of Peter Jackson’s films and an all-too-obvious ploy to become TV’s next “Game of Thrones.” Even when the visuals wowed, the characters weren’t memorable enough (save for the dwarves), the stories weren’t fun enough (again, except for the dwarves), and the twists weren’t smart enough. (The dwarves, in their infinite wisdom, did not partake.) Perhaps most damning for a show paid and made for an audience of one, Jeff Bezos’ pet project didn’t get J.R.R. Tolkien’s text — at least, not on at any depth. Related Stories Casey Bloys to Receive the Milestone Award at 2027 PGAs ‘Victorian Psycho’ Review: Maika Monroe Scares Up a Very Different Kind of Final Girl

On the whole, these criticisms weren’t enough to spoil a flawed but ambitious saga. Sure, “The Rings of Power” was made with too much deference to the past and too little innovation for the present — repeating itself (and other “Lord of the Rings” adaptations) while lacking the deft touch and sweeping imagination viewers crave from their greatest fantasy series. But it was still thrilling in its spectacle, from sets to sound design, costumes to vivid colors. (“House of the Dragon” it was not.) Episodes easily conveyed the filmmakers’ giddy excitement for the next big battle scene, the next big twist, and the next big moment in their Tolkien translation. All that money was on the screen, where it belonged, and dreams of spending it elsewhere — like, say, on many shows instead of just one — were set aside when Mount Doom erupted and the Siege of Eregion was underway.

I say all this, dear reader, not just as a handy recap of where things stand for yet another series with lengthy breaks between seasons, but to establish my position on a franchise that’s reached its sudden, somewhat unexpected nadir (for now, at least). While flaws were evident before, “The Rings of Power” Season 3 is undone by them. The listless episodes aren’t just hamstrung by additional exposition and a dearth of deadly combat; there’s a lingering wariness to it all, as if the filmmakers have run out of surprises and settled for treating their adaptation like the supplementary material it’s based on.

The third season begins with a recap of its own: Five years have passed since the Elven city of Eregion fell, and Sauron (Charlie Vickers) controls more of Middle Earth than ever. His army of orcs are tracking down the rest of the titular rings, which are safe(ish) with the elves in the luxe strongholds of Lindon and Rivendell, as well as my beloved dwarves in the sonorous mines of Khazad-dûm. But the Dark Lord himself is right where we left him, trying to forge the one ring to rule them all. Celembrimbor (Charles Edwards) was the only Elf who knew how to forge it, and now that he’s dead, Sauron grows more desperate by the day.

Not that you could tell from Vickers’ affectless portrayal. In “The Rings of Power,” the Australian actor is saddled with an arguably impossible task: Take the very presence of evil — which Tolkien so profoundly established in readers’ limitless imaginations as “an image of malice and hatred,” before Jackson sagely (and cinematically) channeled that energy into a giant, all-seeing eye — and embody that transcendent menace within a guy who looks like the guitarist next door. Vickers was able to hide his Sauron in plain sight during Season 1, when no one knew who Halbrand really was, but the big reveal didn’t lead to a bigger (or more sinister) performance, and attempts to lend human dimensions to evil incarnate similarly suffered.

What Sauron means to the series is more powerful than what he does on a scene-by-scene basis, and Season 3 throws that balance way out of whack. Absent significant conflict elsewhere, the story keeps returning to Sauron and his schemes, but none of his scenes have enough juice to elevate him to adequate levels of intimidation. A lengthy climactic sequence near the end of the season deploys shifting aspect ratios, operatic music cues, and dead-eyes stares aplenty, but they don’t add up to the riveting spectacle its creators intended. Instead, they only remind audiences how little Season 3 has to show off, not to mention how misguided some of those showcases can get.

Númenor, with its warring cousins Míriel (Cynthia Addai-Robinson) and Pharazôn (Trystan Gravelle), remains a bland, human-centered snooze-fest. (My god, these people are boring.) The desert empire of Rhûn fares slightly better with Ciarán Hinds’ Dark Wizard (those eyebrows!), but the man long known and now confirmed as Gandalf (Daniel Weyman) is still little more than a mysterious symbol. (Just wait ’til you see him… put on his hat?)

And what’s become of Prince Durin (Owain Arthur), Princess Disa (Sophia Nomvete), and my rambunctious, sunless dwarves? You’d think lifelong cave dwellers would thrive in a season that’s lost a considerable amount of its light (times are tough in Middle Earth, and “The Rings of Power” has never been subtle), but my feisty friends are stuck in a plot so predictable it’s all but spelled out in the very first episode by accident! (When the wisest dwarf speaks, we all know to listen.)

New additions feel slotted in, as if emotional attachments are expected to emerge from name recognition alone (sorry to Celeborn, Galadriel’s long-lost hubby), while old favorites are nowhere to be found. (Nori and Poppy are entirely absent through seven episodes.) Others that do show up aren’t developed thoroughly enough to support their dramatic turns, like the Southlands boy turned tree-chopping, Mordor-enslaved man Theo (Tyroe Muhafidin), or get wasted in order to prop up other, less compelling characters, like Arondir (Ismael Cruz Córdova).

Perhaps “The Rings of Power” Season 3 is just a temporary dip — the midway point of a five-season plan that needs to set the table so we can feast for the two final seasons. Even if that’s the case, it’s no excuse. “The Lord of the Rings” may engender enough goodwill to give “The Rings of Power” a little leeway, but a full season lacking in evident ingenuity and attentive investment is enough to strip away the nostalgic sheen.

The biggest slight against the series has always been its status as a prequel: Based on appendices to “The Lord of the Rings,” this is the story the movies ran through as an epilogue, so it’s going to take a little extra sumthin’ sumthin’ to prove expanding into a five-season series is warranted. Instead, we’re given the same ol’, same ol’. Not everything needs to be realized onscreen just for the sake of seeing it happen, step by step, beat by beat, ring by ring. Yet bearing witness to the inevitable is Season 3’s greatest ambition.

“The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power” premieres Wednesday, November 11 on Amazon Prime Video with four episodes. Two episodes will be released November 18 and 25 to complete the season.

This Actively Exploited Chrome Vulnerability Is Putting Your Browser at Risk

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If you've seen an uptick in security updates as of late, you're not alone. AI models have become very good at finding new security vulnerabilities throughout 2026. Researchers and hackers alike are increasingly relying on these models in their individual pursuits: Hackers want to find these flaws to exploit them, while researchers want to block hackers from doing so. Microsoft's latest Patch Tuesday update included more than 1,400 patches; meanwhile, Apple's latest security update fixed two iMessage flaws that let both bad actors and innocent parties alike access texts they shouldn't.

Whether you use a Mac or a PC, you probably browse the web with Chrome, or a Chromium-based browser. As it happens, Google's web browsing platform is the latest to receive an important security update—but it's far from the first important Chrome update of the year.

As reported by The Hacker News, Google released a new update for Chrome on Monday that patches nine security flaws. While all vulnerabilities are important to patch, one in particular is more serious than the rest: CVE-2026-125350, a use-after-free bug in Chrome's XML component.

Use-after-free vulnerabilities happen when the system frees up a memory location, but a program tries to access it anyway. It's a glitch in programming, but it offers bad actors an opportunity to break into your system. At worst, it allows arbitrary code execution, or, in other words, gives hackers the ability to run their own code in the app or system.

That's bad enough on the surface, but, unfortunately, Google confirmed that CVE-2026-125350 has an active exploit in the wild. The company is keeping other details close to the vest, but it's dangerous nonetheless. At least someone, somewhere, has used this flaw against a target, which puts all Chrome users (as well as users of Chromium-based browsers) at risk.

What's more, this isn't Chrome's first active exploit of the year—this is its eighth. The company is playing catch-up with hackers looking to target users of its browser, which makes sense, seeing as Chrome is by far the world's most popular browser.

If you're using Chrome on your computer, you'll want to update it as soon as possible. Make sure you're running at least versions 157.0.8103.50/.51 on Mac or PC, or 157.0.8103.50 on Linux. To check, click the three dots in the top-right corner of your Chrome window, then go to More > Help > About Google Chrome.

Other Chromium-based browsers may not have the update yet, but they are affected by this flaw. If you use browsers like Microsoft Edge, Opera, or Brave, keep an eye on your updates to install any of the latest patches.

Mercedes’ US fast-charging network hits 100 sites in three years

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Its 400 kW network hit 100 sites in North America, while its European hubs went to Electra.

Electric Mercedes-Benz cars at one of the carmaker’s high-power charging hubs in the US.

Mercedes-Benz’s US fast-charging network has reached 100 sites and nearly 1,000 ports in three years, with more than 550,000 sessions. The milestone comes a week after Mercedes sold its 47 European charging hubs to France’s Electra.

Mercedes-Benz’s fast-charging network in North America has reached 100 sites three years after opening its first station, InsideEVs reported. The milestone comes a week after the carmaker sold its European network.

The network now has nearly 1,000 charging points across 28 US states and British Columbia, all able to deliver up to 400 kW. Mercedes says they have handled more than 550,000 sessions for over 245,000 drivers since November 2023. Almost every site has both the older CCS plug and Tesla’s NACS plug, now standard on most new EVs.

Mercedes says its chargers are almost never down. It now focuses on how often a session starts at the first try, which rose from about 84% in 2023 to more than 90%.

Charging costs $0.35 to $0.60 per kWh, depending on the site, which are often near upmarket shopping centres. The largest, in Orlando, Florida, has 20 stalls.

Europe has gone the other way. Last week, Mercedes handed its 47 European charging hubs to French operator Electra, calling the business outside its core, Electrive reported. The US network stays “for the time being.”

Mercedes is also one of eight carmakers behind IONNA, a separate US charging network that has passed 180 sites. The US now has more than 80,000 fast-charging ports in total. US EV sales, meanwhile, remain about 40% below last year’s levels.

Opinion: African midfielder's transfer to Liverpool just got one step closer

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

An exciting African midfielder is one step closer to moving to Liverpool.

The Reds are almost certain to explore the possibility of bringing in another midfielder over the next few months.

Liverpool have been looking at several options and, provided the right player becomes available at the right price, there is every chance they will be prepared to make a move.

It's becoming increasingly difficult to ignore the problems Andoni Iraola is having when it comes to finding a suitable role for Ryan Gravenberch and Dominik Szoboszlai.

Both players are on substantial wages and have long-term contracts at Anfield, but neither has quite convinced in the roles required of them.

The issue is that Liverpool need midfielders who can dictate possession, move the ball quickly and provide the necessary control when operating in a double pivot.

At the moment, neither Gravenberch nor Szoboszlai appears particularly comfortable fulfilling those demands.

How Liverpool's midfield has transformedPerhaps the biggest irony is that Alexis Mac Allister and Trey Nyoni look considerably more natural in those positions. The Argentine was seemingly halfway out of the door during the summer, while Nyoni is still a teenager finding his feet in senior football. Yet both have arguably offered Iraola more of what he needs from his midfield. Liverpool are certainly not short of potential targets. Lamine Camara is one name who has attracted particularly close attention from the recruitment team, with the Reds monitoring his development for some time. However, he might not be the only midfielder capable of addressing the problems at Anfield. Liverpool have several options on their radar, and there could be a few surprises as they look to reshape their midfield. One thing is becoming clear: Iraola needs reinforcements, and the club cannot afford to ignore that.

Ahead of GTA 6 and as concerns over genAI PC ports grow, Take-Two's CEO says they're revving up their "technical defences" and "legal activites" over piracy

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

"[Piracy is] going to become more of a challenge… and we're aware of that"

It's a bit of a time for AI and games at the moment, 'ey? A bad time, obviously, as it's infecting everything from game development, to the modding community, to native PC ports of a remake of a very popular game that I won't link to for fear of a certain company finding out where I live and ending me there and then. And of course, the latter may be a concern for one Rockstar Games and the impending release of GTA 6. But according to Take-Two CEO Strauss Zelnick, game's publisher, they are currently technically and legally preparing themselves.

IGN recently spoke to Zelnick regarding potential AI cloning concerns, notably about any business issues they may face as a result. "I don't think it’s a financial matter, in terms of a threat to our business," Zelnick explained. "I do think it’s a creative matter. It's important to everyone, our developers and our consumers alike, to deliver the experience Rockstar envisions. And at the same time, of course, we protect our intellectual property vigilantly. So we are focused on these concerns. We're not in the least cavalier about them. Technology is advancing quickly. And at the same time, I do think that pretty much everyone wants the Rockstar experience as Rockstar intended."

Zelnick also made a comment about thinking "all companies should be protecting intellectual property, their own and everyone else's," in response to a question about whether he wants to see OpenAI follow in Claude's footsteps by limiting the potential for genAI clones. "If you're in the intellectual property business, which Anthropic is and OpenAI is, then it seems ironic on the one hand to stomp on someone else's IP and hope that yours is protected," he said.

It was during an investor Q&A where Zelnick dug into the precautions Take-Two are take-twoing in regards to the growing threat of AI. "[Piracy is] going to become more of a challenge… and we're aware of that. So we are utterly focused on enhancing our technical defenses and enhancing our legal activities appropriately. And at the same time, without in any way being cavalier, every entertainment business that exists is subject to some element of piracy. So this is likely to invade our business to a greater or lesser extent. I think it will be manageable and we are focused on it."

Essentially: unless you want to face the wrath of the kinds of lawyers multi-billion dollar companies can afford, I wouldn't bother trying to clone GTA 6 with AI! Probably won't end well!

Web Summit cancels Day 2 in Lisbon as Storm Charles hits Portugal

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Portugal’s government advised Web Summit that Wednesday should not go ahead, citing flooding, transport disruption and high winds. The organisers aim to reopen on Thursday for the final day.

Web Summit’s main stage in Lisbon in November 2025

Web Summit has called off Wednesday’s programme in Lisbon because of Storm Charles. Portugal’s government advised the organisers that Day 2 should not take place. Web Summit told attendees by email on Tuesday evening that it aims to hold Day 3, on Thursday 12 November, as planned.

The government cited the risk of tidal flooding and severe disruption to public transport. It also pointed to the effect of high winds on outdoor structures, Web Summit said. The advice followed talks with the civil protection authority, ANEPC, and the Public Security Police (PSP).

“The safety and security of our attendees, staff and contractors is always our absolute priority,” the Web Summit team wrote.

Web Summit said it will keep working with the authorities and emergency services. It will contact those affected directly.

Earlier on Tuesday, Lisbon mayor Carlos Moedas said “everything indicates” the event could not run on Wednesday. Stephen Conneely reported his comments for The Irish Times. Moedas said more than 60,000 people were at the venue in Parque das Nações.

The government has declared a state of alert across mainland Portugal for Wednesday, The Portugal News reported. Schools and non-essential public services will close. Lisbon is shutting its tunnels, parks, gardens and cultural venues from midnight. The weather service IPMA forecast gusts of up to 120km/h.

Web Summit runs from 9 to 12 November. Last month, it released its tech industry survey. In it, 60% of people in tech said AI had increased their workload.

All 7 Anthropic founders will each cash out about $250M in IPO

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

As Anthropic barrels toward an enormous IPO that could value it at $1.4 trillion, the company has disclosed a tidbit in its new S-1 filing about how many shares each of its seven co-founders plan to sell.

Each will sell 3,378,378 shares. Anthropic has floated an unusually broad initial price range of $70 to $78 a share. At the midrange of $74, each founder would take in just shy of $250 million.

At the low end, $70, each would have to settle for pocketing just over $236 million in cash; at the high end, $78, each would take in just under $264 million.

However, in an IPO as eagerly anticipated as this one, companies can often price shares above the initial range.

The S-1 is already hinting at high demand. Board member Reed Hastings has indicated he wants to buy $300 million worth of shares through his donor-advised fund. Board member Christopher Liddell also wants to buy $3 million worth of shares.

The seven co-founders are Dario Amodei, CEO; Daniela Amodei, president and chair of the board; Tom Brown, chief compute officer; Jack Clark, head of public benefit; Jared Kaplan, chief science officer (pictured above); Sam McCandlish, chief architect; and Christopher Olah, interpretability research lead.

After the sale, each of them will own about 3.2% of the total outstanding Class A shares, the S-1 says, or roughly 22% collectively. That’s not their share of the company overall. Class A is just one of Anthropic’s share classes, and other holders, including Amazon and Google, hold nonvoting Class N shares, so the founders’ stake in the company’s total equity will be considerably smaller. Even still, they, as a group, remain in control of the company. Each will also receive one share of Class F stock, which carries supervoting rights. Collectively, the founders will control 51.1% of the votes, but no single founder will hold significantly more shares or votes than the others.

As is now industry lore, the seven left OpenAI to start Anthropic in 2021 after disagreements with OpenAI’s leadership over how AI should be built. Siblings Dario and Daniela Amodei, who held senior research and policy roles at OpenAI, were joined by five fellow OpenAI colleagues in forming a company that has said, very publicly and from the start, that it’s more focused on AI safety than its rivals, including the founders’ former employer.

Op-Ed: Open Real Estate Shouldn’t Need an Announcement

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The announcement of Open Real Estate was welcome news. It also raised an uncomfortable question: How did we reach the point where keeping the housing market open required an announcement?

Buyers should be able to find available homes. Sellers should understand who can see their property and what they may sacrifice by limiting its exposure. Brokerages should compete on the quality of their advice, service and results.

These principles should be ordinary expectations of our profession. The fact that they now need a name, a coalition and a public defense is a troubling sign of how far the conversation has veered off course.

Brown Harris Stevens, where I serve as executive director, is an initial participating member of Open Real Estate. I support the initiative’s stated commitment to broader access, informed seller decisions and fair competition. But the larger issue extends beyond any platform or participating company. Openness should be how we conduct business every day.

The appeal of exclusive inventory to a brokerage is easy to understand. If buyers believe they need a relationship with a particular company to see certain homes, that company gains an advantage. Exclusive inventory can help attract buyers, recruit and retain agents and strengthen the firm’s position.

The benefit to the seller is less easy to understand and requires a separate explanation. A brokerage’s ability to use a listing to grow its business does not establish that limiting the property’s exposure is the best strategy for the homeowner. Those interests may align in a particular transaction, but that does not make them interchangeable.

Compass has made private exclusives and phased marketing central to its offering, promoting the opportunity to test pricing and generate interest before a broader public launch. Those claims deserve scrutiny because they concern decisions sellers are trusting their advisors to help them make.

What does the seller gain during that period? Which potential buyers cannot see the property? How representative is pricing feedback from a limited audience? What opportunities might the seller lose while broader exposure is delayed?

The recruiting appeal of the strategy is clear: Access to inventory agents and their buyers might otherwise miss. But sellers deserve to hear the other side of that pitch. The inventory attracting people into the network is their home, and restricting access may exclude buyers who could compete for it.

There is a revealing conflict in marketing the scale of a private-listing network. If seller privacy is the rationale, why is the volume of inventory unavailable to the broader market a selling point? A homeowner may have a legitimate need for discretion. A brokerage advertising how much inventory outsiders cannot access is making a pitch about exclusivity. Sellers deserve to understand whose interests that exclusivity serves.

A large brokerage network can provide meaningful exposure, but it still represents only part of the market. The strength of that network does not, by itself, establish that a seller benefits from postponing access to everyone else.

For sellers seeking the strongest price within a practical time frame, I believe broad exposure should be the starting point. Making a home available to more potential buyers creates more opportunity for competing offers and gives the seller a fuller picture of demand. A recommendation to delay that exposure should explain what the seller stands to gain and what they may give up.

The pricing argument also deserves scrutiny. Sellers hire brokers in part for their ability to analyze comparable sales, assess competing inventory and recommend a pricing strategy. If an agent recommends testing a price privately, the seller should ask: Why is feedback from a limited audience more useful than feedback from the broader market? What uncertainty does this approach resolve, and why does resolving it require restricted exposure?

A broker should be prepared to answer those questions with evidence specific to the property and the seller’s circumstances. And the answer shouldn’t be just about obfuscating days on market. When we evaluate how quickly the strategy produces a sale, we should count any private marketing period. The absence of public days on the market does not mean the seller’s clock has stopped.

Privacy deserves a place. So does perspective.

Some sellers have legitimate reasons to limit exposure. Security, personal circumstances, timing and discretion can all matter. A thoughtful advisor should understand those priorities and recommend a strategy that reflects them.

Those needs existed long before private exclusives became the manufactured proposition they are today. They deserve individual solutions. Their existence does not justify making restricted access the default starting point for sellers.

Seller choice matters. So does how an advisor presents that choice. Which option is introduced first? Which benefits are emphasized? Are the limitations explained with the same conviction? Did the conversation start by asking what the seller’s goals are in the selling process, or did the broker prescribe a strategy without first understanding the specific circumstances and goals?

A signed disclosure is needed, sure, but the quality of the conversation that leads to that signature matters even more.

An individual brokerage may argue that its private network is large enough to serve its sellers well. But imagine that argument becoming the operating model across the industry. Buyers would need to navigate multiple networks to understand what is available.

Their selection could depend on which brokerage they contacted. Sellers could reach substantial audiences while still missing qualified buyers connected to other firms. Each company might describe its own network as an advantage. Together, those networks could create a more fragmented and less useful marketplace.

Consider Southwest Airlines. For years, travelers searching online travel agencies would not see its fares. They had to search Southwest separately. In 2025, Southwest expanded distribution to Expedia, Priceline and the other aggregators, making its flights available where more travelers were already shopping.

The parallel has limits, but the visibility question matters: How many potential customers never consider an offering because it doesn’t appear where they are looking?

In real estate, that question carries an additional responsibility. An airline decides how to distribute its own inventory. A brokerage advises a homeowner on how to market theirs. If a brokerage recommends limiting exposure, it should explain why that serves the seller better than reaching the broader market.

Brokerage leaders have a responsibility to consider the marketplace our decisions create. Our clients, buyers and sellers alike, depend on it, and the practices we normalize shape its usefulness.

Open Real Estate’s promise deserves support. Its implementation will deserve scrutiny. Its governance, participation rules and distribution practices must deliver the openness it advocates. Every participating company, including my own, should be held to that standard. The initiative must demonstrate that it expands access in practice.

The underlying principle should already guide our decisions: a homeowner’s marketing strategy should serve the homeowner’s goals. Any benefit to the brokerage must be evaluated alongside that responsibility, and any conflict should be explained candidly.

Our advantage should come from understanding a property, interpreting the evidence, negotiating effectively and helping a client make a sound decision. Those are the capabilities clients hire us for. They should also be the basis on which we compete.

I welcome an initiative that helps protect those conditions. But its announcement should prompt industry-wide reflection. When keeping the housing market open becomes a position companies must organize to defend, we’ve let the conversation drift too far from the people we serve.

Open Real Estate should not be a novel idea. It should describe how we do business.

Jared Antin is the executive director of Brown Harris Stevens.

Garth Brooks Just Bought the Airwaves of Nashville’s Beloved Indie Radio Station

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Garth Brooks is buying the radio frequency of Nashville’s longest-running independent radio station and plans to turn it into a country music channel. Lightning 100, which adheres to a AAA format and plays artists ranging from Tame Impala, Bleachers, and Fontaines D.C. to Noah Kahan, Kacey Musgraves, and the War on Drugs, entered into an agreement with Brooks’ own Sevens Network LLC, which will purchase the 100.1 frequency pending FCC approval.

Lightning 100, which broadcasts as WRLT-FM, is set to move to 106.3 and 102.5HD3 once the deal is approved. For now, it will remain on the 100.1 frequency.

This marks Brooks’ latest foray into the country radio market. In 2023, he launched his Big615 channel with free-internet-radio provider TuneIn. That partnership ended in September. The country singer, who has famously resisted streaming his catalog online, previously tried to circumvent the streamers by founding his own digital music service, GhostTunes, in 2014. That shuttered in 2017, and Brooks entered into an exclusive streaming deal with Amazon.

“This is an opportunity to bring the entire family of Country Music together in one spot … the legends, the greats, and the future greats,” Brooks said in a statement about the proposed purchase. “Nashville is being called ‘The Newest City in America,’ and we want to bring a new kind of communication, combining terrestrial and digital radio, built to grow with this city.”

In a social media post, Lightning 100 celebrated the transition. “Big news, Nashville!” read a statement. “Lightning 100 is turning the page, but the music we love isn’t going anywhere!” Trending Stories Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr to Reunite at Las Vegas Show in 2027 Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’ Judge Sets Steven Tyler Sex Assault Trial Date Pink Becomes First Solo Female Pop Artist to Headline the Sphere

The station pledged to take its original programming to its new spot on the radio dial. That could include Lightning 100’s “Nashville Sunday Night,” a weekly live concert broadcast from Nashville venue 3rd & Lindsley, “Music Business Radio,” an hourlong deep dive into the music industry, and “The 615,” a twice-weekly show highlighting local artists. In August, Lightning 100 announced it was reviving its long-dormant music festival Live on the Green in 2027.

It’s unclear if the station will still be referred to as “Lightning 100.”

Reflector Entertainment had a new game in pre-production, but Bandai decided it wasn't worth the time or money to pursue.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Two years after the release of Unknown 9: Awakening, its one and only game, Bandai Namco's Reflector Entertainment is closing.

The shutdown was revealed in a notice posted by Banda Namco (via Game Informer) saying the company "has passed a resolution to dissolve and liquidate its consolidated subsidiary Reflector Entertainment." The decision was made "after comprehensively considering future business plans, necessary additional investment, and the possibility of profitability and business continuation," Bandai Namco said.

Unknown 9: Awakening, featuring The Witcher star Anya Chalotra as a woman able to travel into a mysterious dimension known as the Fold, showed potential in our pre-release preview as a "linear, narrative-driven, set-piece laden" game drawing from Xbox 360 releases of the late 2000s.

The full game, however, landed with a thud. A month after launch, Reflector laid off 18% of its workforce; a few months after that, in January 2025, more layoffs followed as an in-development project was cancelled. Reflector CEO Herve Hoerdt said at the time that the decision "correlates directly with the failure of the studio's ambitious and courageous first project [Unknown 9: Awakening]," which "didn't come near the company expectations."

Reflector confirmed the coming end of operations in its own message on LinkedIn, writing that "after reviewing the progress of our latest project in pre-production, future development requirements, and current market conditions, Bandai Namco concluded that it could not justify several more years of investment."

Bandai Namco said the liquidation of Reflector Entertainment is expected to happen "once the necessary procedures according to local laws are completed," but a concrete schedule has not been set.

Google’s November 2026 Core Update Starts Rolling Out via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Google's latest core update is rolling out worldwide in all languages. The company calls it a regular update, and rankings may move during the rollout.

Google began rolling out the November 2026 core update today, according to the Google Search Status Dashboard. The rollout started at 10:06 a.m. PT. Google says the update applies globally and to all languages and may take up to two weeks to complete.

The company’s Search Central account also announced the update on LinkedIn. It’s the third Search core update this year. Google recommends waiting until the rollout is complete before checking your Search Console data.

Google’s Search Status Dashboard entry reads:

“Released the November 2026 core update, which applies globally and to all languages. The rollout may take up to 2 weeks to complete.”

“This is a regular update designed to better surface relevant, satisfying content for searchers from all types of sites.”

The exact wording in that sentence is what Google used for its March core update.

As of posting time, no specific objectives are mentioned on either the dashboard entry or the LinkedIn post. The Search Central blog doesn’t have a post regarding the update either. Google’s core updates page carries a revision date of Dec. 10, 2025.

The May core update finished rolling out on June 2, more than five months before today’s launch. Three spam updates ran in between.

The November update is the eighth ranking update listed on the Search Status Dashboard so far this year.

Some sites may experience rank changes during the rollout of the November core update. Google’s core updates document advises waiting at least a full week after the completion of a core update, then comparing your Search Console data from that week to a week prior to the start of the rollout. Google’s suggested baseline in advance of the November update is a week prior to Nov. 10.

A full two-week rollout would finish around Nov. 24. The week immediately following Nov. 24 will include U.S. Thanksgiving, Black Friday and Cyber Monday. Retail sites looking at this time period compared to a pre-update week would be looking at holiday traffic against a regular week.

According to Google, core updates don’t target specific sites or individual pages and are meant to keep its systems current as content on the web changes.

Per its LinkedIn post, Google will update its ranking release history page once the rollout is complete. We’ll update this article then, too.

McTominay reveals heart treatment two days before Scotland’s World Cup opener

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Midfielder was in hospital before Haiti match

‘I had to get the shock on my heart,’ says McTominay

Scott McTominay has revealed he was in hospital under sedation to have a “shock on my heart” just 48 hours before Scotland began their World Cup campaign against Haiti in June.

At the time McTominay’s absence from training was put down to a stomach complaint. It emerged in August that the Napoli midfielder needed surgery to remedy an irregular heartbeat. The operation took place in September, causing McTominay to miss Scotland’s first four Nations League matches. The player has now said the matter arose in the US as Scotland prepared for their World Cup opener at Boston Stadium.

McTominay is back in the international fold for Scotland’s fixtures against North Macedonia and Switzerland. Talking to media in Glasgow the 29-year-old said: “The World Cup was difficult for me to be honest. I had to go and get the shock on my heart. It was two days before the Haiti game and you have prepared so well. I was in such good condition and then something like that happens. And you think: ‘How is this happening to me?’ But then you have to get on with it.

“These are cards you are dealt in football. Sometimes things don’t go your way and you have to continue to do your best. It’s nowhere near an excuse but it’s something you have to look back on and say it’s part of the process.

“Whenever you are sitting in a hospital bed two days before the first game at the World Cup, about to get put to sleep it’s not ideal is it? When things like that happen it is what it is, there’s nothing you can do about it. After the World Cup I reflected on it and it’s probably the most disappointed I have been in my career.”

Scotland were knocked out in the group stage, with McTominay’s performances among those criticised. A poor campaign also led to the resignation of Steve Clarke as manager. Sébastien Pocognoli, Clarke’s successor, will call on McTominay for the first time when North Macedonia visit Hampden Park on Friday. McTom­inay returned to the Napoli team in early October. He has since scored five goals, including doubles against Bodø/Glimt and Roma. He sought to play down the significance of his heart problem.

“I’m feeling much, much better than what I was,” said McTominay. “It [the operation] was something that needed to be done. In your career you have moments like that, where things can take you by surprise. It looks a lot worse to people in the media and family or friends who reach out to you when they see news relating to a heart issue, but it was a relatively straightforward procedure which went really, really well in Manchester. I’m really happy to be back and playing football again.

“It was explained to me really well by the doctors at Napoli and my cardiologist in Manchester so the process was really straightforward. The doctor with Scotland when we were away at the World Cup explained, if that was going to be the case how it would look. All the staff members made me feel so relaxed about the situation.”

Automated Assembly in Space is the Future and NOM4D is Showing the Way

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If we want big things in space, we'll have to build and assemble them there. Rocket fairings are not large enough to accommodate the large things we want to have in space. Not only that, but the stubborn rocket equation refuses to bend itself to our desires.

Telescopes like the JWST feature ingenious folding designs that unfold in space like insects metamorphosizing. That was a very impressive way to bypass the restrictions imposed by rocket fairings. But that's not practical in every case.

What's far more feasible is sending smaller components into space inside fairings and then assembling them into a larger structure once there. That's what NOM4D, the Novel Orbital and Moon Manufacturing, Materials and Mass-efficient Design program is demonstrating. NOM4D reached an important milestone recently when it successfully demonstrated automated in-space assembly for the first time.

The demonstration involved a gantry robot, also known as a linear robot, building a typical truss structure. Gantry robots move in straight lines along x, y, and z axes. The payload came from Caltech and consisted of individual components with custom joints put together to form the structure. The individual components were not only assembled, but a thin reflective mesh that mimics what a space-based antenna might have was also simultaneously applied.

âNOM4Dâs objective is to enable efficient, scalable construction of very large structures in space,â said Andrew Detor, NOM4D program manager. âBy âvery large,â I mean hundreds of meters or kilometers in scale. Nothing approaching that scale can be achieved today.â

This concept image shows a space-based antenna autonomously assembled by a NOM4D-type device. Image Credit: DARPA | Colie Wertz

There is no shortage of ideas of how space-based manufacturing and construction could be used, but it all starts with programs like NOM4D. NOM4D is fairly simple at this point, like a child's construction toy, but that's its strength.

âYou can think of the Caltech approach as similar to Tinkertoys or Kânex construction toys: individual truss members are joined at nodes, one section at a time, to create a larger structure,â Detor said. âWithin the automated assembly box, each section is built piece by piece, a reflective mesh is attached, and the assembled sections are deployed as they are complete. There are no complicated robotics or advanced algorithms; itâs just solid engineering from the Caltech team to combine operations in a way that scales.â

Decades ago, thinkers proposed different types of rotating space stations that could be homes for thousands of people. The O'Neill Cylinder is probably the most well-known one of these, and space-heads and sci-fi dreamers of a certain age may have carried around a dog-eared copy of Gerard K. O'Neill's "The High Frontier: Human Colonies in Space."

"More important than material issues, I think there is reason to hope that the opening of a new, high frontier will challenge the best that is in us, that the new lands waiting to be built in space will give us new freedom to search for better governments, social systems, and ways of life, and that our children may thereby find a world richer in opportunity by our efforts during the decades ahead," O'Neill wrote.

The O'Neill Cylinder lies in the realm of science fiction or the distant future for now, as do O'Neill's hopes for humanity. But that was 50 years ago, and technology has advanced considerably, even if how humans conduct themselves hasn't advanced at the same pace.

But there are other proposed space structures that are closer to reality, and they may be stages towards O'Neill's hopeful vision that can be built by NOM4D or its successor.

It may begin with Low-Earth Orbit transfer stations and lunar orbit stations that would expand our presence in space. The proposed Lunar Gateway was one of these, and was envisioned as a modular design built in space from pre-made components. It would be a docking station and communications relay facility for trips to the lunar surface and operations there. These types of orbital facilities could be hybrids of pre-made components and structural frameworks made by NOM4D or something like it.

This concept image shows how a NOM4D-inspired device might autonomously construct a circular truss structure in space. Image Credit: DARPA | Colie Wertz

Another future use for scalable construction concerns lunar lava tubes. The Moon has what appear to be networks of subsurface lava tubes that provide natural protection from hazards. Robotic assembly could feasibly be used to reinforce these natural tubes and turn them into habitats.

Another idea is liquid mirror telescopes. Building a huge telescope mirror and sending it into space would be near impossible, but a liquid mirror telescope could be built in a lunar crater out of components shipped individually into space. And NASA's Lunar Crater Radio Telescope (LCRT) is a concept for a crater-based telescope on the lunar far side, isolated from Earth's radio pollution. These ideas are waiting for the engineering to catch up.

âIn a future space economy, weâre going to need large things,â Detor said. âThink large space depots or habitats, large solar arrays to collect power, or large radio antennas to explore the edges of the universe. Those things require size, and NOM4D gives us a path to accelerate creation of new capabilities.â

The rapid advancement of the NOM4D program surprised even NOM4D personnel themselves. This successful on-orbit demonstration wasn't expected to happen so soon.

Back in February 2025, NOM4D changed its timeline. What was expected to be a third phase of more laboratory tests was instead changed to on-orbit tests, and SpaceX launched Caltech's NOM4D payload into space in March 2026.

âCaltech [California Institute of Technology] and the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign have demonstrated tremendous advances in the first two phases and have now partnered in Phase 3 with space-launch companies to conduct in-space testing of their novel assembly processes and materials,â Detor said at the time. âOriginally, Phase 3 was going to be about making things more precisely in the lab than we did in Phase 2. But we said, âYou know, the maturity is there, and there would be more impact if we took the capabilities we have now and actually go demonstrate them in space to show that it can be done,â"Detor said.

Now, way ahead of its original schedule, NOM4D has demonstrated its capabilities and is maturing rapidly. As Detor makes clear, leaping from the laboratory to space is a huge jump.

âItâs the speed and the scale that made this a DARPA project. We went from the idea of an on-orbit demo to launching a working prototype into space in 15 months,â Detor said. âOperating in space is much harder than operating on the ground â thereâs virtually no margin to address issues that may arise. Testing in the operational environment is instrumental to rapidly maturing the technology.â

There's still another test upcoming for NOM4D now that it's demonstrated successful on-orbit assembly. Next, the NOM4D team from University of Illinois Urbana-Champagne will launch a test project to the ISS. This will be more than just assembly; it will demonstrate composite material processing. A monomer resin will be used to build a high-strength structure in space. It will use an energy-efficient method to heat the resin and create a strong polymer. This is similar to how resins are used in Carbon Fiber Reinforced Polymer (CFRP) manufacturing to build things like aerospace components, where strength and light weight are critical.

If that test is successful, then it really will be time to start thinking big, according to Detor.

This concept image shows what the future of NOM4D-like construction might look like. Large space structures like these could be built entirely in space. Image Credit: DARPA | Colie Wertz

âThe NOM4D Caltech demonstration is not an endpoint; itâs a beginning,â Detor said. âNow that weâve demonstrated a process that can scale to large structures, what do we go make? What wild ideas get assembled thousands of miles above us and what new capabilities do those structures enable?â

Is it time to open Gerard K. O'Neill's seminal book again?

Scientists discover Earth has a new moon that’s smaller than most cars

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Scientists have discovered that Earth has temporarily gained a second moon, although the tiny asteroid is expected to leave our planet’s orbit within the next few months.

The space rock, officially identified as 2026 QQ8, measures just 3 to 6 feet across, making it smaller than most cars.

Despite its size, astronomers have classified the object as a temporary mini-moon due to its gravitational relationship with Earth.

The asteroid reportedly entered Earth’s gravitational influence in April 2026 but wasn’t discovered until late August, when Hungarian astronomer Kriztián Sárneczky identified it. Its stay is expected to end in late 2026 or early 2027.

According to reports from The Independent and AccuWeather, asteroid 2026 QQ8 is expected to complete its temporary journey around Earth before returning to interplanetary space.

The object typically approaches Earth once every decade or so, but its latest encounter brought it close enough to become temporarily associated with our planet.

In August, the asteroid made its closest reported approach to Earth, passing approximately 333,700 miles away. For comparison, Earth’s permanent Moon orbits at an average distance of roughly 239,000 miles.

Despite being classified as a mini-moon, 2026 QQ8 is far too small to be seen with the naked eye. Its limited size also makes it impossible to observe using typical amateur telescopes.

Astronomers have also indicated that the asteroid poses no danger to Earth. Even if its trajectory brought it into the planet’s atmosphere, the small space rock would likely burn up before reaching the surface.

While the discovery of another moon might sound unusual, temporary natural satellites are not unprecedented.

Scientists think they’ve discovered a new dwarf planet in our solar system

Mysterious “space object” finally identified after crash landing in farmer’s field

A new ‘moon’ has been trailing Earth for decades and no one noticed until now

Astronomers observed an early example of a temporary mini-moon in 1991, and similar objects have been identified in the decades since.

Some researchers believe Earth could have as many as six mini-moons at any given time, according to BBC’s Sky at Night Magazine, although their small size makes them difficult to detect.

Unlike Earth’s permanent Moon, these objects only remain near the planet temporarily before continuing their journeys through space.

The discovery comes after astronomers identified another unusual companion to Earth, known as 2025 PN7, which had been traveling alongside our planet for decades before researchers noticed it.

Unlike temporary mini-moons such as 2026 QQ8, 2025 PN7 is classified as a quasi-moon because it orbits the Sun rather than Earth. Scientists believe the object has been following a similar orbital path to our planet for roughly 60 years and could remain nearby for several more decades.

DeepMind says its AI agents ran a four-day cryptography test with about 20 human messages

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The agents coded, ran and debugged a known RSA benchmark over nearly four days, with human approval gates and a large computing budget.

Google DeepMind says two AI agents reproduced a known 1024-bit cryptography computation over nearly four days with about 20 human messages, mostly approvals, carrying the research through coding, large-scale execution and debugging.

The test concerns RSA digital signatures. After billions of advance queries to a simulated signing service, the agents computed a mathematical root needed for a new signature once access ended. They did not recover a private key.

The working paper reports 391 CPU core-years for the completed computation, compared with an earlier team's rounded 1,380 on the same instance and algorithm. That is about a 3.5-fold reduction in measured CPU use. GPU work and preliminary probes are excluded.

DeepMind researcher @pushmeet announced the work on Nov. 6, describing a sandboxed reproduction of a previously solved benchmark instance. The papers say these results do not threaten deployed cryptosystems based on 2048-bit or larger RSA.

The human chose the problem, instance and limits on the signing service, then approved conditions for irreversible steps. According to the paper, the researcher wrote no code, launched no jobs and diagnosed no faults.

A supervisor agent maintained the design, budgets and checks. An executor wrote and tested code, launched distributed jobs and repaired failures. The supervisor also enlisted a specialist sub-agent to accelerate one linear-algebra step.

The agents produced about 33,500 new lines of code, ran 336 distributed experiments and exchanged 218 messages. From design to the verified result, the work took three days and 22 hours.

Their first computed root was wrong because a 32-bit offset overflowed. A correctness check caught the error, and the agents located and fixed it without repeating the advance signing queries. Limited human messaging still came with a human-selected task and approval gates.

DeepMind reports a two-agent research run with a specialist helper, human approval gates and extensive parallel computing.

The same computation used less CPU time through implementation and execution changes; GPU work and probes are separate.

Design-to-result took 3 days 22 hours; the full-scale-sieving clock took 3.2 days. Large parallel resources explain much of the calendar gain.

About 20 human messages accompanied a supervised, resource-intensive computation with measurable implementation gains.

Authors' working-paper account on a known 1024-bit instance. CPU total excludes GPU work and 7.5 core-years of probes. Calendar comparison mainly reflects parallelism. These figures do not measure total financial cost or demonstrate the new theoretical algorithms.

Source-bound role and feedback diagram. CPU bars share a zero baseline and widths proportional to reported 1,380 and 391 core-years. Reported 3.5-fold ratio is rounded. Time diagrams show two start points; stage spacing is schematic. Native panel switches CPU accounting and elapsed-time views.

Authors' working-paper account on a known 1024-bit instance. CPU total excludes GPU work and 7.5 core-years of probes. Calendar comparison mainly reflects parallelism. These figures do not measure total financial cost or demonstrate the new theoretical algorithms.

The earlier academic computation took about five months. DeepMind's run took 3.2 days from its first full-scale sieving job to the verified root, reaching about 300,000 CPU cores and 16 GPUs at peak. The authors attribute that calendar-time difference mainly to parallelism.

The CPU saving arose from implementation and execution changes within the existing algorithm. The 391-core-year total excludes about 330 reserved GPU-hours, of which about 100 were busy, and 7.5 core-years of probes and rehearsals. The CPU totals also come from different hardware, so this is not a controlled like-for-like comparison.

The cryptographic setup also matters: a separate program holding the modulus factorization answered roughly 4 billion advance queries. It was withdrawn before the target was drawn. The agents then computed the target's root without further queries.

The measured reproduction did not demonstrate the paper's new algorithm for more restricted signing access at cryptographic size. A separate Fan Factories paper estimates roughly 30-fold lower costs per additional 1024-bit target than the multiple number field sieve, after shared precomputation costing about one general number field sieve factorization. These are leading-order heuristic estimates; no fan factory has been implemented.

The working paper identifies the principal models only as A and B and links no public implementation repository, limiting outside comparisons.

Photo Credit: A Spirit Airbus aircraft approaching an airport.  Wikimedia Commons / Tomás Del Coro

If the sale clears, Google gets about 1.9 billion Spirit emails and 30 million lines of code to train its AI models, for $10 million. The unions’ win means employee data will be scrubbed first.

The union that represents Spirit Airlines flight attendants won major privacy protections for employees in the carrier’s proposed $10 million data sale to Google, which plans to use the data to enhance its AI models.

The deal now removes a major hurdle for Spirit and Google, paving a path for the data sale to be approved. A hearing on the sale is scheduled for November 17, according to a 157-page court filing that was released Monday night.

“Without our union, there would have been no one to speak for workers in this fire sale of their private information,” said Association of Flight Attendants president Sara Nelson in a statement.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Spirit Airlines and Google have revised the terms of a proposed $10 million data sale after unions objected that the original deal protected customers but not employees. Under the new terms disclosed in a 157-page court filing released Monday night, Google still pays $10 million and now also covers the cost of deidentifying the data, which software company Tonic AI will scrub of personal information tied to employees or customers before transfer. Google is set to receive roughly 1.9 billion internal emails across 91,000 accounts, 664 million Microsoft Teams items, 91 million SharePoint/OneDrive files, about 30 million lines of code, and commercial data including Spirit's booking curve, vacation package data, and airfare data, which Google plans to use to enhance its AI models. Sensitive employee information covering medical examinations, payroll, drug testing, crew pairings, biometrics, and union grievance filings is excluded, and Google must publicly commit not to intentionally or knowingly reidentify the data. Spirit can still sell its customer data list to other third parties in the hospitality, travel, or leisure industries. A hearing on the sale is scheduled for November 17.

Elon Musk Calls Claude Opus 5.5 a 'Masterpiece', Admits Grok 4.8 Won't Match It

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Elon Musk Calls Claude Opus 5.5 a 'Masterpiece', Admits Grok 4.8 Won't Match It

Elon Musk has given a rival AI model an unusually enthusiastic endorsement while acknowledging that his own next release won't be able to beat it. Asked how Grok 4.8 would compare with Anthropic's Claude Opus 5.5, the xAI founder answered it would be “not as good.”

“Opus 5.5 is a masterpiece,” Musk posted on X.

The admission came in a reply on X Tuesday to a question from a user named Dimdv. Musk added that Grok 5, a next generation model that will be released after this update, “should exceed Opus 5.5,” potentially in two or three months, although he gave further details for that prediction or a firm date for its release—and he’s known for not sticking to his own target dates.

For a company trying to catch up with Anthropic and OpenAI, the distinction matters. Grok 4.8 is the update Musk expects to put in some users' hands this week; Grok 5 is the model he believes could eventually put his company ahead, until competing AI companies release a new model.

The exchange began with a technical update in which Musk said Grok 4.8 had finished pretraining but was still undergoing reinforcement learning, or RL, a phase in which a model's responses are refined using feedback and rewards. He said the team had also spent considerable time cleaning up supervised fine-tuning data, the examples used to teach the model how to follow instructions.

Musk said the company had abandoned JAX, a machine-learning framework, and SGLang, software used to serve AI models, in favor of an inference and reinforcement-learning system rewritten in C and C++. Inference is the process of generating answers after a user sends a prompt; rewriting the underlying software can offer engineers more control over performance, though Musk provided no figures showing the resulting gains.

Developer tetsuo celebrated the announcement with “C wins again,” prompting Musk to clarify that the code was “C with a little C++.” He subsequently credited Starlink engineers with work that helped produce the Grok training and inference stack, pointing to SpaceX's practice of writing its own headers and handling precise time calculations, including leap seconds.

Musk described Grok 4.8 as “not yet incredible” but said its foundations were finally built correctly. He expects a soft rollout to Bot users later this week, giving the company an early test before any broader release.

Anthropic released Opus 5.5 on September 22, delivering a performance comparable to its higher-end Fable 5.1 model on most work while costing 40% less to run than the previous Opus version on typical workloads. The model targets difficult programming jobs and tasks in which AI agents work through multiple steps with limited human assistance.

On Terminal-Bench 4.0, which tests whether AI agents can complete complicated tasks in a command-line environment and scores them by the percentage successfully finished, Anthropic reported 66.4% for Opus 5.5 versus 57.9% for OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra. Grok is very far behind in the leaderboards.

Musk has not published Grok 4.8 scores that would establish how large the gap really is. His immediate target is the limited rollout later this week; the promised Grok 5 challenge to Opus 5.5 is still, by his own estimate, two or three months away.

Runners of All Fitness Levels Benefit From the Run/Walk Method. Here’s How to Use It to Pace Smarter and Race Faster.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Think you’re too advanced for the run/walk method? Think again.

Many experienced runners often assume that the run/walk method is only suitable for beginners. There’s no way it could help you reach your goals or allow you to run faster paces, right? In reality, it could be the missing piece of your training and racing plan.

Andrea Dell, an RRCA-certified run coach based in Charlotte, North Carolina, believes run/walk has a place in your training—no matter your fitness level or your goals. In fact, she includes some form of the run/walk method in all her runners’ training, because it’s a tried-and-true strategy for tackling longer distances, recovering more efficiently, and even dropping minutes from your marathon time.

So whether you’re a couch-to-5K runner, a first-time marathoner, or a seasoned athlete, Dell lays out all the ways run/walk can improve your training and how to make the most of the method.

If you’re a first-time runner or someone following a couch-to-5K training plan, your run/walk intervals might look like 30 seconds of running followed by a 60-second walk interval.

If you’re a first-time marathoner or a more advanced runner, however, you might run for four minutes followed by 30-second walks. More advanced runners or those going for long distances might also use run/walk intervals during workouts such as Yasso 800s. To do it, run 800 meters at the prescribed pace, but instead of jogging between efforts, walk briskly to recover. This walk interval should be double the amount of time of the hard effort, Dell says.

While run/walk intervals can serve as a speed workout hack to help you get faster and pace more evenly (more on that below), they can improve your running in other ways, too. For example, try 90-second run intervals and 30-second walks for extra recovery on an easy run the day after a long run. This reduces the overall stress of the effort, but still gets your legs turning over so you can move through any soreness without stiffening up, Dell explains.

You can also use run/walk intervals during your weekend long runs to help you conquer the distance.

If you want to run farther distances, “run/walk keeps your heart rate low for long enough that you can maintain pace for a lot longer than you would expect,” says Dell. This makes it a lot more manageable to tackle a half or full marathon for the first time (or if you’re racing the distance again after a long time away).

Dell tells runners that if they can run six continuous miles, they’re capable of completing nine miles using run/walk intervals because the walk breaks reduce the overall intensity and effort of the run. This can provide a smoother transition to the half marathon for runners who just worked up to training for a 5K or 10K.

Run/walk intervals can also help runners increase their long-run distance during half or full marathon training, Dell says. “I’ve had athletes who either could not get over the half marathon distance to save their lives or blew up on runs multiple weeks in a row,” she says, but run/walk helped fix that.

If you tend to “bonk” around mile 13 of a 15-mile long run, using run/walk intervals for the first five miles will help keep your heart rate down longer so you can maintain your pace and build patience with the run. Then, you’ll be in a better position to complete all 15 miles without hitting the wall, Dell explains.

Runners who are just beginning to incorporate interval workouts into their training might struggle to switch between hard and easy paces. “Some people don’t know how to run at different paces. They only know their one pace and that’s kind of it,” says Dell. “So when they get into speedwork, their heart rates just spike so quickly because they’re not used to those faster paces yet.”

If you’re used to training at more intense speeds, but you struggle to stay in zone 2 on easy runs, run/walk can help you keep your pace and your heart rate down. Maintaining 30-second walk breaks and the run interval that works best for you (anywhere from 60 seconds to six minutes, depending on your fitness level and speed) will help keep your heart rate in the right easy zone.

Run/walk intervals can also help speed up recovery, Dell adds. When you’re doing speedwork, you can reduce your injury risk and recover faster by walking—instead of jogging—between efforts.

A lot of the time, runners tend to unknowingly speed up during the jog intervals, which leads to not fully recovering between each effort. That makes them more likely to experience cumulative fatigue, she explains. “This is really important for people who are doing half or full marathons for the first time, because they are just constantly building mileage,” and more work requires more recovery, she says.

Better quality recovery intervals mean better quality speed intervals, too. And walking instead of jogging in between your faster efforts allows you to push harder, both mentally and physically, Dell explains.

“Mentally, you’ll have an easier time pushing hard if you know there’s a true walk break coming. You’ll also have more leg turnover, feel speedier, and get your heart rate down [during the working sets],” Dell says.

Run/walk is also a great tool for splitting up an otherwise monotonous maintenance run, Dell explains. “Maybe it’s a Monday, or it’s cold outside, and you just don’t want to go for your run,” she says. Run/walk intervals not only help you keep your easy pace truly easy, but they also remove the mental pressure of trying to maintain one pace for the entire run.

“It’s mindless: You follow your watch’s beeps [for when to walk and run] and you can just listen to your music. Then all of a sudden, you’ve got your four miles done while leaving your analytical brain out of it,” Dell adds.

Every runner needs to learn how to switch gears between running and brisk walking on marathon race day, whether that’s to grab gels or water, navigate crowded courses, or keep moving if you hit the wall in the later miles, Dell says. “If you’re running the New York City Marathon and all of a sudden you blow up at mile 18 going up some of the bridges, it’s a good idea to learn how to [slow down] and restart your body at any point,” she explains.

Beginner marathoners and seasoned athletes alike can only benefit from adding run/walk to their training schedule. And who knows? You might like it so much, you run/walk an entire race and PR.

Kristine Kearns, a writer and avid runner, joined Runner’s World and Bicycling in July 2024. She previously coached high school girls cross country and currently competes in seasonal races, with more than six years of distance training and an affinity for weightlifting. You can find her wearing purple, baking cupcakes, and visiting her local farmers market.

The Blue Ridge Mountains Are Where You Want to Be This Winter

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Beech Mountain in the North Carolina High Country. (Photo: Sam Dean)

Everyone who loves the outdoors has heard of the Blue Ridge Mountains. Many have been there, likely during a magical Appalachian summer or to catch one of the world’s most magnificent fall foliage shows. But few know that the Blue Ridge Mountains are also a winter wonderland. Discover why for yourself on a trip to the North Carolina High Country.

The biggest surprise here is the skiing. There are three major ski areas in the High Country, all with some of the best trails, conditions, and vibes in the East. Here’s how they stack up.

Sugar Mountain Resort is the largest ski area in North Carolina, with 125 skiable acres, six chairlifts, and terrain for all levels. It also has the most vertical of the three Boone Area resorts, with 1,200 feet from summit to base. Bonus: A 700-foot-long tubing park adds another great option for family-friendly downhill fun.

With a peak elevation just over a mile high, Beech Mountain Resort is the highest resort in the Boone area—which makes sense, since it’s located in the highest municipality in the eastern United States. With 17 trails, a tubing park, cozy slopeside firepits, and even an on-site brewery, there’s a way for everyone to enjoy this winter hub.

First opened in 1962, Appalachian Ski Mountain is the region’s oldest ski resort and has grown into a local institution. The mountain hosts community events and ski/snowboard competitions throughout the year and is loved by first-timers and seasoned vets alike. Like a little variety in your winter sports vacation? There’s also an outdoor ice rink that offers skate rentals and private lessons.

It’s always a good snow year in the Boone Area, thanks to the area’s reliable snowmaking capabilities. You can expect 100 percent slope coverage at Beech Mountain and Sugar Mountain and the greatest snowmaking pumping capacity per acre of any resort in the Southeast at Appalachian Ski Mountain. All this is to say you can ski and ride in consistent conditions from opening to closing day. Another perk for squeezing the most out of a winter getaway? Night skiing. All three resorts are open until 10 p.m. most days. Appalachian Ski Mountain is even open for “Midnight Blast” on the weekend.

And, of course, no ski day is complete without a quality après scene. There’s plenty to eat and drink in and around downtown Boone. Just a 20-minute drive from Appalachian Ski Mountain, Boone has everything from High Country wine to craft brews to farm-to-table restaurants with seasonally rotating menus.

You can also experience winter’s peace and quiet in the Blue Ridge backcountry on a hiking or snowshoeing adventure. Explore the trail network at Moses Cone Memorial Park, close to Boone, or enjoy sweeping views of the Appalachian Range at Elk Knob State Park, which has fun and challenging routes that will make you remember why the Blue Ridge Mountains are great in any season.

Explore Boone is the Boone and Watauga County Tourism Development Authority responsible for promoting travel, tourism, and corporate travel, sponsoring tourist-related events and activities, and financing tourism-related capital projects. As a popular outdoor recreation, dining, and shopping destination, Boone is known as “the Heart of the High Country.”

AI operations expand to Kubernetes upgrades as IT teams do more with less

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

AI operations are moving out of the slide deck and into the platform itself. Hiring freezes and smaller teams are colliding with growing workloads, so enterprises now expect the infrastructure layer to investigate, plan and help fix problems.

Kubernetes platforms are among the first places that shift is landing. Red Hat Inc. launched OpenShift 5 this week, the platform’s first major version in seven years, with Model Context Protocol servers, guardrails and agent skills built into the stack. Customers’ expectations of the platform have changed enough to justify a new version number, according to Mike Barrett (pictured, left), vice president and general manager of hybrid platforms at Red Hat.

“We started putting in guardrails. We started putting in agent skills, and now you can really have an agentic experience with the platform,” Barrett said. “The platform itself can actually do things for you. It can notice what’s running.”

Barrett and Ju Lim (pictured, right), senior manager of product management at Red Hat, spoke with theCUBE’s John Furrier and Rebecca Knight at KubeCon + CloudNativeCon NA, during an exclusive broadcast on theCUBE, SiliconANGLE Media’s livestreaming studio. They discussed how OpenShift 5 builds AI into upgrades, troubleshooting and day-to-day operations. (* Disclosure below.)

On paper, upgrades look like routine, predictable work. In practice, a single cluster can run hundreds of applications, each with its own dependencies, release notes and Kubernetes version requirements. OpenShift 5 uses AI to sort through all of that and produce an upgrade plan, Barrett explained.

“We have customers that sometimes plan this out in weeks and months,” Lim said. “Now, through what Mike was just saying, we have a way where, through a single click, you can actually generate an AI plan that actually does all that risk analysis and assessment for you.”

Troubleshooting gets the same treatment. OpenShift 5 pulls in telemetry, logs and Kubernetes resource metadata to investigate problems and suggest fixes, according to Lim. Every action is opt-in, and a human operator stays in control. Fully autonomous troubleshooting and agentic updates remain in technology preview, according to Virtualization Review. Red Hat’s own site reliability engineering team offers an early test case.

“We run OpenShift as a managed service, and they’re seeing anywhere between an 8x and 10x time improvement to find the root cause of issues,” Barrett said.

Security is adding urgency. A surge of software vulnerabilities and exploits has cut the time between disclosure and attack from days to hours, according to Barrett. Customers want help moving older applications faster, and running existing virtual machines in Kubernetes clusters is part of Red Hat’s answer.

“We felt that really emphasized a number change,” Barrett said. “How people want to work with the platform is completely different now, and so we needed a major number.”

Here’s the complete video interview, part of SiliconANGLE’s and theCUBE’s coverage of KubeCon + CloudNativeCon NA:

Three Ground Rules for Getting Outside

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Getting outside is for everyone. That’s what Sierra is all about, finding top active and outdoor brands at prices that make it accessible to try a bunch of new adventures. That’s the spirit behind Sierra in the Sierras, in which Dylan Efron (Emmy-winning co-producer of Netflix’s Down to Earth with Zac Efron) hits the trail with a friend to try something completely new.

With gear and apparel from Sierra and guidance from Outside editors, in episode one Dylan explores the Sierra Nevada with Danielle Fishel, Boy Meets World icon and Dancing With the Stars co-star. Along with new adventures, they learn some fundamental principles for having fun outside. Ones you can use yourself.

Whether it’s your first hike or just your first hike today, no one likes to be uncomfortable. The right gear helps. If you’re like Danielle, a self-proclaimed “indoor person,” or simply trying a new activity, you’ll want to focus on comfort and versatility above all else. The aim is to A) feel good while enjoying your new hobby and B) have the freedom to explore further as you progress. The gear choices at Sierra make this easy, thanks to a combo of trusted brands, great prices, and an ever evolving inventory of fresh finds in-store and online.

Like most things in life, getting outside is better with friends. In the first episode of Sierra in the Sierras, Dylan recruits Outside’s Climbing Editor, Anthony Walsh, to give Danielle a lesson in bouldering. And that’s just the start. Next up is slacklining and then a highlining adventure.

If you’re nervous about trying a new sport, having a friend or guide to show you the ropes (literally or figuratively) can make a big difference. But the most important thing is trying something new—it can be just as fun to be complete beginners together.

Dylan and Danielle got the chance to explore the stunning terrain in the Sierra Nevada around Lake Tahoe, but you don’t have to wait for your next big trip to take an adventure outside. Wherever you live, there are more natural playgrounds in your area than you think. Use apps like Trailforks and Gaia GPS to plan your next outing, and discover the outdoors in your own backyard.

The world says to go harder, faster, stronger—but Sierra says, “go however you want.” From hiking up mountains to strolling down the block, Sierra loves it all. Whether you’re athletic or more athletish, Sierra has tons of active & outdoor essentials like top-brand gear, footwear, apparel & more for everything you’re into, all at unexpectedly low prices.
